[36m[PLAN][0m feat.compile.numeric_permissions: `@numeric(...)` on a function or a block grants numeric permissions to the code written in it, arms, loop bodies and lambdas included, never to a function it calls (design principles 2.13; docs/design/op-semantics 6.3, 6.4). The graph keeps them in a per-node column beside the source positions; std::rgir shows them as a `numeric` attribute on the nodes that have any. Rewrites keep them, CSE and tensor::fuse do not merge nodes granted different ones, and the CPU back ends compute the canonical results with or without them.

[36m[PLAN][0m feat.mm.gc_elder_exhaustion: 
Programs that allocate more small arrays than the elder space holds (32 MB) inside the element
functions of nested map, filter, reduce and foreach. An allocation that found the elder space full
used to run a mark-sweep on the spot, which freed objects held only by C++ locals (an Array whose
backing store was being allocated); the next collection then traced the freed object, or the
program computed with reused memory. Every test checks the printed total against a value computed
independently of Camel.

Under std::tf a collection could also run while a flow was running: the other workers were inside
operators, and a map held the results of its finished elements outside any frame. The std::tf
tests cover that: the nested map at full size, and every program at a small size with a major
collection, heap verification before and after, at every safepoint the scheduler polls.

[36m[PLAN][0m feat.mm.gc_pacing: 
How often the allocator asks for a collection when a program allocates large objects, and how
much the large-object space holds at most. One array of 40 MB stays alive while 200 temporary
arrays of 0.8 MB each are allocated and dropped, one per call (190.7 MB in all). After a sweep
the budget is the larger of the floor (CAMEL_GC_LARGE_BUDGET_MB, default 4 MB) and
CAMEL_GC_GROWTH_PERCENT percent of the volume the sweep left, so the space holds at most
live * (1 + percent / 100) plus the allocation that crossed the budget, and the number of
collections is the volume allocated divided by the budget. Both are read from the allocator's
statistics line (CAMEL_GC_STATS=1); nothing here depends on time, and the program runs one node
at a time, so the numbers are the same on every run.

[36m[PLAN][0m feat.mm.capacity: 
Programs of ordinary size must not run into the capacity of the spaces the compiler keeps the
program in. The graph, permanent and meta spaces (runtime graphs; types and layouts; constants
and function objects of the graphs being compiled) grow by segments up to a configurable total
(CAMEL_GRAPH_SPACE_LIMIT_MB, CAMEL_PERM_SPACE_LIMIT_MB, CAMEL_META_SPACE_LIMIT_MB, 256 MB each
by default); reaching it is the located diagnostic RuntimeDiag::MemoryExhausted, which names the
space, the usage, the limit and the variable.

The programs are generated by test/tools/scale/scale.py: N small functions of one shape
(integer arithmetic, an `if`, a string literal, an array literal, a lambda passed to `map`, or
all in turn) and a `main` that prints the sum of their results, which the generator knows
without running Camel. Before the spaces could grow, and while every graph that owns a constant
took a 256 KB block of the 16 MB meta space, 62 such graphs were the limit: 60 functions with an
`if`, a string, an array or a lambda, or 3051 arithmetic functions (their 62 callers), ended in
an unlocated std::bad_alloc (progen finding F24).

[36m[PLAN][0m feat.mm.gc_soak: 
Soak and scale tests of both collectors, in the sizes that fit the default test set (the
minute-long ones are in test/plans/perf/gc.plan.toml).

gc_soak allocates and drops without end around a live set that stays the same; the memory the
collector holds, read from its statistics line (CAMEL_GC_STATS=1), must not depend on how long
the program runs. gc_live_large keeps tens of megabytes of small objects alive, more than the
first elder segment holds. gc_prl_blocked_worker runs a parallel map in which one worker sits
in a long operator, so the stop-the-world handshake gives up, while the others allocate far
more than the birth space holds. The last tests set a heap limit that the program exceeds and
check the diagnostic.

[36m[PLAN][0m feat.mm.gc_poison_sweep: 
The handle rules (docs/technical/36_gc_handle_rules.md) checked against what the other plans
exercise: their tests are run again with safepoint poisoning (CAMEL_GC_POISON=1), in which every
safepoint moves every young object and overwrites where it was, so a C++ pointer kept across a
safepoint, a missing root or a stale interior pointer ends the program with a report instead of
going unnoticed. The assertions of each test are kept: the program must print what it prints
without the mode.

The plans are named by directory, so an operator or a module added later is covered as soon as
it has a test there. Left out: the slow model tests of autodiff and onnx (run them with
`CAMEL_GC_POISON=1 node scripts/test.js feat/modules/autodiff.plan.toml` when touching those
modules), tests that choose their own collector settings, benchmarks and differential tests.
A test that cannot run in the mode says `sweep = false` and why.
